### 226. assistant

**Updated structure per your latest feedback.**

### Summary of the change
- `archives/` is now the **umbrella** directory for all historical/completed material.
- Inside it, `past_projects/` is the specific bucket for completed **small tech debt / starter projects** (low-regret-moves and similar bounded initiatives go here once they are terminal).
- `checkpoints/` lives directly under `archives/` so it stays prominent as the dedicated trail for major checkpoints (perfect for regression detection as you do quality iterations in the near future).
- Other categories (decisions, research, audits) live at the `archives/` level.

This keeps the live `docs/` top level very clean while giving you a clear, classified historical area.

### Final proposed tree (after the moves on the branch)

```
docs/
├── README.md
├── BACKLOG.md
├── CODE_MAP.md
├── COMMERCIAL_READINESS_REVIEW.md
├── EVALUATION.md
├── GOLDEN_SET_IMPLEMENTATION.md
├── GOLDEN_SET_PHASE1_VIDEOS.md
├── GOLDEN_SET_VENDORING.md
├── HOW_RALLY_DETECTION_WORKS.md
├── ROADMAP.md
├── TRACKNET_WSL_SETUP.md
├── VIDEO_RECORDING_GUIDELINES.md
│
└── archives/                              ← umbrella
    ├── README.md                          ← umbrella policy
    │
    ├── past_projects/                     ← for completed small tech debt / starter projects
    │   ├── README.md                      ← explains the "past_projects" classification
    │   └── low-regret-moves/
    │       └── LOW_REGRET_MOVES_PLAN.md   ← STATUS: COMPLETED
    │
    ├── checkpoints/                       ← regression detection trail (major milestones)
    │   └── 2026-06-scaffolding-complete/
    │       └── README.md                  ← STATUS: COMPLETED + purpose + what comes next
    │
    ├── decisions/
    │   └── DECISIONS.md
    │
    ├── research/
    │   ├── AUDIO_E1_FINDINGS.md
    │   ├── AUDIO_RALLY_DETECTION_PLAN.md
    │   └── RALLY_DETECTION_RESEARCH.md
    │
    └── MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md
```

### Key policy excerpts (enforcing your rules)

**`docs/archives/README.md`** (umbrella):
```markdown
# archives/

Umbrella directory for all **historical and completed** material.

## Classification inside archives/
- `past_projects/` — Completed small tech debt, starter projects, or low-regret initiatives.
- `checkpoints/` — Snapshots of major project states (especially useful for detecting regressions in quality, priorities, or baselines over time as we do quality iterations).
- `decisions/`, `research/`, etc.

## Archival Rule
Only move something into `archives/` when the work is in a clear **terminal state**:
- DONE
- COMPLETED
- REJECTED

Never archive in-flight or pending work.
```

**`docs/archives/past_projects/README.md`**:
```markdown
# past_projects/

This subdirectory is for **completed small tech debt, starter projects, or low-regret initiatives**.

## Criteria for past_projects/
- The work was a bounded "project" (e.g. low-regret moves, scaffolding maturation, specific tech debt cleanup).
- It has reached a terminal state (STATUS: DONE / COMPLETED / REJECTED).
- It is no longer the active focus.

Future small completed starter projects or tech debt cleanups that are similar in nature should land here.
```

**Example STATUS header** (in the archived low-regret plan):
```markdown
# Low-Regret Moves Plan — Config Model First

**STATUS: COMPLETED** (all items merged June 2026 — PRs #49, #50, #51)
```

The 2026-06 scaffolding checkpoint under `archives/checkpoints/` is already seeded with the same `**STATUS: COMPLETED**` pattern and explicitly calls out that future quality iterations will add more dated checkpoints here. This gives you the clean "doc trail for regression detection" you want.

- Make any other naming or placement tweaks?

Just say the word. This version should feel exactly right with `archives/` as the umbrella and `past_projects/` as the classification for low-regret-style starter/tech-debt work.

### 245. tool_result

1→# Session Management
2→
3→Grok saves every conversation to disk automatically. Whether you work in the TUI, in headless mode, or over agent stdio, Grok records the exchange as a session. You can resume, rewind, or compact it. This document describes how to manage sessions.
4→
5→---
6→
7→## What Sessions Are
8→
9→A session is a persistent conversation with full history. It includes:
10→
11→- All user prompts and agent responses
12→- Tool calls and their results
13→- TODO/task list state
14→- File snapshots for rewind
15→- Token usage and turn counts
16→- Subagent sessions (when enabled)
17→
18→Sessions are identified by a unique session ID (a UUIDv7 when Grok generates it; a client may supply its own ID with `-s`) and stored on disk under `~/.grok/sessions/`. Set `GROK_HOME` to override the base directory; when it is unset, Grok uses `~/.grok`.
19→
20→---
21→
22→## Storage Layout
23→
24→Grok stores each session in its own directory, grouped by working directory. It URL-encodes the working directory to name the group. When the encoded name exceeds 255 bytes, it instead uses a slug plus a hash and records the original path in a `.cwd` file inside the group.
25→
26→```
27→~/.grok/sessions/<encoded-cwd>/<session-id>/
28→  summary.json            # metadata: summary/title, timestamps, model ID, message counts
29→  updates.jsonl           # ACP session update stream (conversation + tool calls)
30→  chat_history.jsonl      # raw chat messages sent to the model
31→  plan.json               # TODO/task list state
32→  rewind_points.jsonl     # file snapshots for /rewind undo
33→  signals.json            # session signals (token usage, tool/turn counters)
34→  feedback.jsonl          # user feedback and ratings
35→  compaction_checkpoints/ # saved state from compaction (manual or auto)
36→  subagents/              # per-subagent metadata (meta.json); the child sessions live in the normal sessions tree
37→```
38→
39→`summary.json` is the index entry. It records the session summary and generated title, the model ID, the creation and update timestamps, the message counts, and a parent session reference for forked or restored sessions. `updates.jsonl` is the authoritative conversation log that drives `/resume` and session restore.
40→
41→---
42→
43→## Starting and Ending Sessions
44→
45→### New Session
46→
47→The TUI creates a new session each time you launch. To explicitly start fresh mid-session:
48→
49→```
50→/new
51→```
52→
53→This clears the current context and begins a new conversation. Alias: `/clear`.
54→
55→### Exit
56→
57→End the session and quit Grok:
58→
59→```
60→/quit
61→```
62→
63→Alias: `/exit`. To leave the current session but stay in Grok, use `/home` to return to the welcome screen.
64→
65→---
66→
67→## Resuming Sessions
68→
69→### From the TUI
70→
71→Use the `/resume` command to browse and resume previous sessions:
72→
73→```
74→/resume
75→```
76→
77→This opens a session picker that lists recent sessions for the current workspace. Select a session to resume it. The command takes no arguments.
78→
79→To switch between, rename, or close the sessions that are currently active (the parent session and any forks), use `/sessions` instead.
80→
81→### From the Command Line
82→
83→Resume a specific session by ID:
84→
85→```bash
86→grok --resume <session-id>
87→```
88→
89→Run `grok --resume` without an ID to resume the most recent session for the current directory.
90→
91→### From the Welcome Screen
92→
93→When you launch `grok`, the welcome screen lists recent sessions for the current directory. Select one to resume it.
94→
95→---
96→
97→## Forking and Renaming Sessions
98→
99→### Fork
100→
101→Branch the current session into a peer agent that starts from a copy of the conversation:
102→
103→```
104→/fork [--worktree|--no-worktree] [directive]
105→```
106→
107→Pass an optional `directive` to set the new session's first prompt. Use `--worktree` or `--no-worktree` to choose whether the fork runs in a new git worktree; omit both to be asked each time. The `--at <turn>` flag is not supported in this version.
108→
109→### Rename
110→
111→Rename the current session's title:
112→
113→```
114→/rename <title>
115→```
116→
117→Alias: `/title`.
118→
119→---
120→
121→## The /rewind Command
122→
123→`/rewind` undoes recent changes by restoring files to their state at an earlier point in the conversation. Use it to recover from mistakes.
124→
125→```
126→/rewind
127→```
128→
129→When you run `/rewind`, Grok:
130→
131→1. Shows a list of rewind points (one per user prompt)
132→2. Lets you select which point to rewind to
133→3. Restores all files to their state at that point
134→4. Truncates the conversation history to that point
135→
136→File snapshots are recorded at each prompt, so you can go back to any previous state.
137→
138→**Important:** `/rewind` modifies files on disk. The changes it reverts are lost unless you have them in git.
139→
140→---
141→
142→## The /compact Command
143→
144→`/compact` compresses the conversation history to save context window space. Use it in long sessions where early messages are no longer relevant.
145→
146→```
147→/compact
148→/compact [context]
149→```
150→
151→The optional `context` argument lets you provide additional instructions about what to preserve during compaction.
152→
153→### Auto-Compact
154→
155→Grok automatically compacts the conversation when the context window approaches its limit. You will see a notification when auto-compact triggers. The `context_window` setting on your model configuration controls when this threshold is reached.
156→
157→---
158→
159→## The /session-info Command
160→
161→View details about the current session:
162→
163→```
164→/session-info
165→```
166→
167→This shows:
168→
169→- Session title (when set)
170→- Shell version
171→- Session ID
172→- Working directory
173→- Model (with a model hash for coding models)
174→- API backend and sandbox profile (when set)
175→- Context window usage (used and total tokens, with the percentage used)
176→
177→---
178→
179→## Headless Session Management
180→
181→In headless mode, you manage sessions through command-line flags:
182→
183→```bash
184→# New session each time (default)
185→grok -p "Hello"
186→
187→# Resume an existing session by ID (errors if it does not exist)
188→grok -p "Continue where we left off" -r <session-id>
189→
190→# Continue the most recent session in the current directory
191→grok -p "What were we doing?" -c
192→```
193→
194→In headless mode, resume an existing session with `-r`/`--resume`, which errors if the session does not exist, or continue the most recent session in the current directory with `-c`/`--continue`. Pass the session ID from JSON output (see below) to `-r`. The `-s`/`--session-id` upsert flag applies to interactive runs only; headless runs ignore it.
195→
196→To read the session ID back, request JSON output:
197→
198→```bash
199→grok -p "Hello" --output-format json | jq -r '.sessionId'
200→```
201→
202→---
203→
204→## Agent stdio Session Management
205→
206→When building with ACP, sessions are managed via protocol methods:
207→
208→```typescript
209→// Create new session
210→const { sessionId } = await connection.request("session/new", {
211→  cwd: "/path/to/project",
212→  mcpServers: [],
213→});
214→
215→// Load existing session
216→await connection.request("session/load", {
217→  sessionId: "existing-session-id",
218→  cwd: "/path/to/project",
219→  mcpServers: [],
220→});
221→```
222→
223→The agent persists all session updates automatically. Clients can reconnect and load previous sessions by ID.
224→
225→---
226→
227→## The grok sessions Subcommand
228→
229→List or search sessions from the command line. `grok sessions` requires a subcommand:
230→
231→```bash
232→# List recent sessions for the current directory
233→grok sessions list
234→
235→# Limit the number of results (default 20)
236→grok sessions list --limit 50
237→
238→# Search sessions by keyword (matches titles and prompts)
239→grok sessions search "rate limit"
240→```
241→
242→`grok sessions list` shows sessions for the current working directory, grouped by worktree label. Each row lists the session ID, the creation and update dates, the source status, and the summary. `grok sessions search` combines a local SQLite index with remote results.
243→
244→---
245→
246→## Worktree Sessions
247→
248→When working with subagents or session forks, Grok can create isolated git worktrees per session. Each worktree gets its own copy of the working directory, so file changes in one session do not affect another.
249→
250→Worktree sessions are managed internally through the `x.ai/git/worktree/*` extension methods. Key operations:
251→
252→- **Create**: Create a new worktree for an isolated session
253→- **Apply**: Merge worktree changes back into the main working directory
254→- **Remove**: Clean up a worktree when the session is done
255→
256→Resume a session in a fresh worktree with `grok -w -r <session-id>`.
257→
258→---
259→
260→## Session Storage Details
261→
262→### Persistence Format
263→
264→Grok stores the conversation as newline-delimited JSON (JSONL). Each line in `updates.jsonl` is a self-contained ACP session update event. This format supports:
265→
266→- Incremental writes (append-only during a session)
267→- Efficient streaming reads (for session restore)
268→- Easy debugging (each line is valid JSON)
269→
270→The smaller state files -- `summary.json`, `plan.json`, and `signals.json` -- are plain JSON rather than JSONL. JSONL is the source of truth for session content; `grok sessions search` additionally maintains a local SQLite FTS5 index over session titles and prompts for fast keyword search.
271→
272→### Session Metadata
273→
274→`summary.json` records, among other fields:
275→
276→- `info` -- the session ID and working directory
277→- `session_summary` and `generated_title` -- the session summary and its model-generated title
278→- `created_at` and `updated_at` -- creation and last-update timestamps
279→- `num_messages` and `num_chat_messages` -- update and chat-message counts
280→- `current_model_id` -- the model in use
281→- `parent_session_id` -- the source session for a fork or restore
282→- `agent_name` -- the agent definition active when the session was last saved
283→
284→### Disk Usage
285→
286→Rewind point snapshots (copies of modified files) are the largest contributor to disk usage in sessions that modify many files. Use `/compact` to reduce history size.
287→
288→---
289→
290→## Tips
291→
292→- Use `/new` to start fresh when your current context is no longer relevant.
293→- Use `/compact` proactively in long sessions to keep the context window effective.
294→- Use `/rewind` to undo mistakes; it restores actual file snapshots instead of relying on the agent to reconstruct earlier state.
295→- In headless mode, capture the `sessionId` from JSON output and pass it to `-r` to build multi-step automations that maintain context.
296→- Check `/session-info` to see how much of your context window has been used.
297→

### 246. tool_result

1→# Cross-Session Memory
2→
3→Memory lets Grok recall facts, decisions, and patterns from earlier sessions. Grok indexes the information you save and searches it automatically, so a new session can reuse relevant context.
4→
5→---
6→
7→## What Is Memory?
8→
9→Without memory, each Grok session starts fresh: the model knows nothing about previous sessions. When you enable memory, Grok can:
10→
11→- Recall project conventions you explained before.
12→- Reuse debugging steps that worked.
13→- Carry architectural decisions forward across sessions.
14→- Avoid re-asking questions it already has answers to.
15→
16→Memory is experimental and disabled by default.
17→
18→---
19→
20→## Enabling Memory
21→
22→### Per-Session Flag
23→
24→```bash
25→grok --experimental-memory
26→```
27→
28→### Environment Variable
29→
30→```bash
31→export GROK_MEMORY=1
32→grok
33→```
34→
35→### Config File (Persistent)
36→
37→```toml
38→# ~/.grok/config.toml
39→[memory]
40→enabled = true
41→```
42→
43→### Force-Disable
44→
45→To disable memory even when other settings enable it:
46→
47→```bash
48→grok --no-memory
49→```
50→
51→Or:
52→
53→```bash
54→export GROK_MEMORY=0
55→```
56→
57→The `--no-memory` flag has absolute highest priority and always disables memory.
58→
59→### Mid-Session Toggle
60→
61→Toggle memory on or off during a session without restarting:
62→
63→```
64→/memory on
65→/memory off
66→```
67→
68→The toggle is session-scoped -- it does not persist to `config.toml`. Toggling off removes access to memory tools but keeps existing files on disk. Toggling on re-initializes memory storage and registers the memory tools.
69→
70→You can also toggle from inside the `/memory` modal by pressing `t`.
71→
72→### Priority Order
73→
74→1. `--no-memory` CLI flag (always disables)
75→2. `--experimental-memory` CLI flag (enables)
76→3. `GROK_MEMORY` env var: `1`/`true` enables, `0`/`false` disables
77→4. `[memory]` section in config.toml
78→5. Remote settings (server-side defaults)
79→6. Default: disabled
80→
81→---
82→
83→## How Memory Is Stored
84→
85→Memory is stored as Markdown files under `~/.grok/memory/`:
86→
87→| Location | Scope | Description |
88→|----------|-------|-------------|
89→| `~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md` | Global | Facts that apply across all your projects |
90→| `~/.grok/memory/<project-slug>-<hash8>/MEMORY.md` | Workspace | Project-specific conventions and context |
91→| `~/.grok/memory/<project-slug>-<hash8>/sessions/` | Sessions | Per-session summaries and logs |
92→
93→Grok suffixes each workspace directory with a short hash of the repository's identity. The identity is the `origin` remote in `org/repo` form when the directory is a Git repository with an `origin` remote, or the directory path otherwise. Because clones and worktrees of the same repository share an `origin` remote, they also share one memory directory.
94→
95→An SQLite index supports hybrid search across all memory files:
96→- **FTS5** provides full-text search for keyword matching.
97→- **vec0** provides vector search for semantic similarity. Vector search is optional and requires an embedding.
98→
99→---
100→
101→## Automatic Saves
102→
103→When a session ends, Grok saves a structured metadata summary to that session's daily log. The summary contains:
104→
105→- Message counts (user, assistant, and tool results).
106→- Topics: the first few substantive user prompts from the session, up to five.
107→- The session date and time (UTC).
108→
109→Grok builds the summary from conversation metadata without an LLM call, without added latency. Grok skips the save for trivial sessions -- those with fewer than three substantive prompts, or fewer than 50 bytes of user text.
110→
111→The summary does not record tool usage, file paths, or shell commands. The session ID forms part of the log filename. To turn automatic saves off, set `session.save_on_end = false`. For richer capture of decisions, patterns, and reasoning, use `/flush`.
112→
113→---
114→
115→## Saving Rich Knowledge with /flush
116→
117→For richer capture -- decisions, patterns, debugging workflows, API discoveries -- use `/flush` in the TUI:
118→
119→```
120→/flush
121→```
122→
123→This triggers an LLM-generated summary of the current session's most important content and writes it to a dated session log. The summary is indexed and searchable in future sessions.
124→
125→Use `/flush` when you want to preserve important context:
126→- Before compaction (which discards old conversation turns)
127→- At the end of a productive debugging session
128→- After discovering important patterns or conventions
129→
130→---
131→
132→## Working with Memory
133→
134→### Remember
135→
136→Ask Grok to remember something, and it appends the note to a `MEMORY.md` file -- the workspace file for project-specific items, or the global `~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md` for cross-project preferences:
137→
138→```
139→> remember to always open PR links after pushing
140→```
141→
142→Grok records entries as durable statements under organized headings, such as `## Preferences`, `## Project Context`, or `## Debugging`. The file watcher reindexes the change on the next memory search, so the new entry is searchable within the current session.
143→
144→You can also save a note directly with the `/remember` command:
145→
146→```
147→/remember always open PR links after pushing
148→```
149→
150→Run `/remember` with no text to enter remember mode, where the next line you type becomes the note. Either way, Grok opens a review panel showing the note (with an optional rewritten version you can toggle with `Tab`); the note is written only after you confirm. On save, Grok shows `Memory saved to ~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md`.
151→
152→### Forget
153→
154→Ask Grok to forget something, and it finds and removes the matching entry:
155→
156→```
157→> forget the snake_case convention
158→```
159→
160→Forget is best-effort: the model searches memory and removes entries that match. For guaranteed removal, edit the files under `~/.grok/memory/` directly and delete the entry yourself. To locate a file, open the `/memory` browser and press `y` to copy its path.
161→
162→### Recall
163→
164→Ask what Grok remembers:
165→
166→```
167→> what do you remember?
168→```
169→
170→Grok searches across all memory files and summarizes what it knows, grouped by source: global preferences, project-specific knowledge, and session history. Use `/memory` to browse the raw files.
171→
172→### Direct Editing
173→
174→You can edit memory files directly under `~/.grok/memory/`. The file watcher reindexes your changes on the next memory search. Use `/flush` to save the current session now, and `/dream` to consolidate session logs into organized topics.
175→
176→---
177→
178→## Browsing Memory with /memory
179→
180→The `/memory` command opens a modal showing all memory files:
181→
182→```
183→/memory
184→```
185→
186→Files are grouped by scope:
187→- **Global** -- cross-project memory (`MEMORY.md`).
188→- **Workspace** -- project-specific memory (`MEMORY.md`).
189→- **Sessions** -- per-session summaries, in reverse chronological order.
190→
191→The modal uses a split-pane layout: the file list on the left, a read-only content preview on the right. The preview updates as you move through the list.
192→
193→### Keyboard Shortcuts
194→
195→| Key | Action |
196→|-----|--------|
197→| `↑`/`↓` or `j`/`k` | Move through the file list |
198→| `PgUp`/`PgDn` | Jump 10 entries |
199→| `/` | Filter the file list |
200→| `y` | Copy the selected file's path to the clipboard |
201→| `x` | Delete the selected session file (press `x` again to confirm) |
202→| `t` | Toggle memory on or off |
203→| `Ctrl+F` | Toggle fullscreen |
204→| `Esc` | Close the modal, or exit filter mode |
205→
206→The preview pane is read-only. Scroll it with the mouse wheel or by dragging its scrollbar. You can delete only session files, not the global or workspace `MEMORY.md`.
207→
208→When the memory modal's content area is under 80 columns, the modal hides the preview pane and shows the file list only.
209→
210→You can also open `/memory` from the command palette.
211→
212→---
213→
214→## Memory Notifications
215→
216→When you save a note with `/remember`, Grok confirms in the scrollback:
217→
218→```
219→Memory saved to ~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md
220→```
221→
222→Background saves — flush, dream, and session-end — run silently and do not post a scrollback message. Use `/memory` at any time to browse what Grok has stored.
223→
224→---
225→
226→## Dream Consolidation with /dream
227→
228→The `/dream` command consolidates scattered memory fragments into organized topics:
229→
230→```
231→/dream
232→```
233→
234→Dream reorganizes individual session logs and memory entries into a coherent, deduplicated knowledge base, which reduces noise and improves search quality over time. `/dream` requires memory to be enabled.
235→
236→### Auto-Dream
237→
238→Dream also runs automatically. By default, Grok checks the consolidation gates when a session ends and runs Dream once enough time has passed and enough sessions have accumulated:
239→
240→```toml
241→[memory.dream]
242→enabled = true     # Run automatic consolidation (default: true)
243→min_hours = 4      # Minimum hours between consolidations
244→min_sessions = 3   # Minimum sessions since the last consolidation
245→# check_interval_secs is unset by default, so Dream runs only at session end.
246→# Set it to a positive number of seconds to also check on a periodic interval.
247→```
248→
249→---
250→
251→## How Memory Affects Prompts
252→
253→### First-Turn Injection
254→
255→On the first turn of each session, Grok automatically searches memory for content relevant to the current project and injects it as context. This means Grok starts with knowledge from previous sessions without a reminder.
256→
257→First-turn injection can be configured:
258→
259→```toml
260→[memory.initial_injection]
261→enabled = true     # Enable or disable first-turn injection
262→min_score = 0.0    # Optional score threshold; unset by default, which applies no filtering
263→```
264→
265→### After Compaction
266→
267→Memory is also searched after auto-compaction to recover relevant context that may have been discarded.
268→
269→---
270→
271→## Memory Search
272→
273→Grok searches memory automatically, but you can also trigger searches manually in the chat:
274→
275→```
276→Search memory for "auth middleware patterns"
277→Read my workspace MEMORY.md
278→```
279→
280→The model has access to two memory tools:
281→- `memory_search` -- Hybrid search across all memory (vector + full-text)
282→- `memory_get` -- Read a specific memory file by path
283→
284→### Hybrid Scoring
285→
286→Memory search uses a weighted combination of:
287→- **Vector similarity** (semantic) -- weight: 0.7
288→- **BM25 text similarity** (keyword) -- weight: 0.3
289→
290→Results are filtered by a minimum score threshold (default: 0.35).
291→
292→### Source Weights
293→
294→Each memory source has a weight multiplier applied to its score. All sources default to `1.0`, and you can adjust any of them under `[memory.search.source_weights]`:
295→
296→| Source | Weight | Description |
297→|--------|--------|-------------|
298→| `workspace` | 1.0 | Project-specific memory |
299→| `session` | 1.0 | Session logs |
300→| `global` | 1.0 | Cross-project memory |
301→
302→### Temporal Decay
303→
304→Session memories decay over time so recent sessions are prioritized:
305→
306→```toml
307→[memory.search.temporal_decay]
308→enabled = true           # Enable time-based decay
309→half_life_days = 7.0     # Score halves after this many days
310→```
311→
312→Only session chunks decay. Global and workspace memories are exempt since they contain curated long-term knowledge.
313→
314→### MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance)
315→
316→MMR re-ranking penalizes redundant results to improve diversity:
317→
318→```toml
319→[memory.search.mmr]
320→enabled = false          # Opt-in diversity re-ranking
321→lambda = 0.7             # 0.0 = max diversity, 1.0 = pure relevance
322→```
323→
324→---
325→
326→## CLI Commands
327→
328→The `grok memory` command manages memory from the shell. It has one subcommand, `clear`:
329→
330→```bash
331→# Clear workspace memory (MEMORY.md, sessions/, and index.sqlite). This is the default scope.
332→grok memory clear
333→
334→# The same scope, stated explicitly
335→grok memory clear --workspace
336→
337→# Clear the global MEMORY.md
338→grok memory clear --global
339→
340→# Clear both workspace and global memory
341→grok memory clear --all
342→
343→# Skip the confirmation prompt (-y is the short form)
344→grok memory clear --yes
345→```
346→
347→To edit memory from the shell, open the files in your editor directly -- for example, `$EDITOR ~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md`.
348→
349→---
350→
351→## Configuration Reference
352→
353→### Core Settings (`[memory]`)
354→
355→| Key | Default | Description |
356→|-----|---------|-------------|
357→| `enabled` | `false` | Enable memory |
358→| `session.save_on_end` | `true` | Write metadata summary on session end |
359→| `watcher.enabled` | `true` | Watch `~/.grok/memory/` for external edits and reindex |
360→
361→### Index Settings (`[memory.index]`)
362→
363→| Key | Default | Description |
364→|-----|---------|-------------|
365→| `max_chunk_chars` | `1600` | Maximum chunk size in characters |
366→| `chunk_overlap_chars` | `320` | Character overlap between chunks |
367→
368→### Embedding Settings (`[memory.embedding]`)
369→
370→| Key | Default | Description |
371→|-----|---------|-------------|
372→| `provider` | `"api"` | Embedding provider (currently `"api"`) |
373→| `model` | `"embedding-beta-3-small"` | Embedding model name |
374→| `dimensions` | `1024` | Embedding vector dimensions |
375→
376→### Search Settings (`[memory.search]`)
377→
378→| Key | Default | Description |
379→|-----|---------|-------------|
380→| `max_results` | `6` | Maximum search results |
381→| `min_score` | `0.35` | Minimum relevance score |
382→| `vector_weight` | `0.7` | Weight for vector similarity |
383→| `text_weight` | `0.3` | Weight for BM25 text similarity |
384→
385→### Initial Injection Settings (`[memory.initial_injection]`)
386→
387→| Key | Default | Description |
388→|-----|---------|-------------|
389→| `enabled` | `true` | Enable first-turn memory injection |
390→| `min_score` | unset | Score threshold for first-turn results. When unset, Grok applies no threshold, which is equivalent to `0.0`. |
391→
392→### Dream Settings (`[memory.dream]`)
393→
394→| Key | Default | Description |
395→|-----|---------|-------------|
396→| `enabled` | `true` | Enable automatic Dream consolidation |
397→| `min_hours` | `4` | Minimum hours between consolidations |
398→| `min_sessions` | `3` | Minimum sessions since the last consolidation |
399→| `stale_lock_secs` | `3600` | Seconds before a stale consolidation lock is reclaimed |
400→| `check_interval_secs` | unset | Periodic check interval in seconds. When unset, Dream runs only at session end. |
401→
402→### Flush Settings (`[compaction.memory_flush]`)
403→
404→You configure flush under `[compaction]`, not `[memory]`, because it is a compaction behavior. Remote settings can override these defaults.
405→
406→| Key | Default | Description |
407→|-----|---------|-------------|
408→| `enabled` | `true` | Enable the pre-compaction memory flush |
409→| `soft_threshold_tokens` | `4000` | Token headroom before the compact threshold that triggers a flush |
410→| `max_flush_write_chars` | `8000` | Maximum characters the flush may write to memory |
411→| `flush_model` | unset | Model for the flush turn. When unset, Grok uses the session's primary model. |
412→| `idle_timeout_secs` | unset | Idle seconds before a background flush. When unset, flush runs only before compaction. |
413→| `semantic_dedup_threshold` | unset | Cosine-similarity threshold for de-duplicating flushed content. When unset, defaults to `0.92`. |
414→
415→### Pruning Settings (`[compaction.pruning]`)
416→
417→You configure pruning under `[compaction]`, not `[memory]`, because it is a compaction behavior. Remote settings can override these defaults.
418→
419→| Key | Default | Description |
420→|-----|---------|-------------|
421→| `enabled` | `true` | Enable tool-result pruning |
422→| `keep_last_n_turns` | `3` | Number of recent turns whose tool results are never pruned |
423→| `soft_trim_threshold` | `4000` | Character threshold above which old tool results are soft-trimmed |
424→| `soft_trim_head` | `1500` | Characters kept from the start of a soft-trimmed result |
425→| `soft_trim_tail` | `1500` | Characters kept from the end of a soft-trimmed result |
426→| `hard_clear_age_turns` | `10` | Turn age after which tool results are replaced with a placeholder |
427→
428→---
429→
430→## Memory Staleness
431→
432→When a session memory is old, Grok attaches a staleness note to it in search results. Older results get a stronger reminder to verify the current state before you rely on them. These notes help you spot stored facts that might no longer be accurate. Global and workspace memories never receive staleness notes, because they hold curated long-term knowledge.
433→
434→---
435→
436→## File Watcher
437→
438→By default, Grok watches `~/.grok/memory/` for external file changes. If you edit memory files directly (e.g., in your editor), the changes are picked up automatically on the next memory search:
439→
440→- Created or modified files are reindexed.
441→- Deleted files have their stale chunks removed from the index.
442→
443→```toml
444→[memory.watcher]
445→enabled = true    # default
446→```
447→
448→---
449→
450→## Troubleshooting
451→
452→### Memory Not Working
453→
454→1. Verify memory is enabled: check `grok inspect` output.
455→2. Check the flag: `grok --experimental-memory` or `GROK_MEMORY=1`.
456→3. Check for `--no-memory` or `GROK_MEMORY=0` overriding your config.
457→
458→### Memory Not Appearing in Sessions
459→
460→Memory is injected on the first turn. If you started a session before enabling memory, start a new session with `/new`.
461→
462→### Viewing Memory Files
463→
464→Use `/memory` in the TUI to browse all memory files with a preview. You can also access them directly:
465→
466→```bash
467→ls ~/.grok/memory/
468→cat ~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md
469→$EDITOR ~/.grok/memory/MEMORY.md
470→```
471→
472→### Debug Logging
473→
474→```bash
475→RUST_LOG=debug GROK_LOG_FILE=/tmp/grok.log grok
476→grep "memory" /tmp/grok.log
477→```
478→

### How to "archive" this session with a name (so you can resume later)

Sessions are **automatically persisted** to disk (under `~/.grok/sessions/`, grouped by working directory). Giving it a good title is the practical way to "archive" it for easy later discovery.

Run these commands in the TUI right now:

1. **Give it a descriptive name** (this sets the title that appears in the session picker, summaries, `grok sessions list`, etc.):
   ```
   /rename docs-declutter-archives-past_projects-quality-iterations-2026-06
   ```
   (or any shorter name you prefer — e.g. `docs-archives-pr54` or `docs-structure-cleanup`)

2. **Note the details** (highly recommended):
   ```
   /session-info
   ```
   This shows the **Session ID** (a UUID), the title you just set, working directory, context usage, etc. Copy the ID somewhere if you want bulletproof resume later.

3. When you're ready to leave:
   ```
   /quit
   ```
   (alias: `/exit`)

The full history (including the previous compaction) is already saved in `updates.jsonl` inside the session directory. The title makes it trivial to find again.

### How to resume it later

- Just run `grok` in this directory (`/home/avidullu/projects/Verifiers/khelsutra/tmp` or the `badminton-highlight-indexer` subdir). The welcome screen shows recent sessions for this workspace — your named one will appear with the title you gave it.
- Inside the TUI: `/resume` (opens a picker for this workspace).
- From the shell:
  - `grok --resume` (most recent for current dir)
  - `grok --resume <the-uuid-from-session-info>` (exact session)
- `grok sessions list` (or `grok sessions search "declutter"`) to find it from the command line.

You can also fork it later with `/fork` if you want to branch from a specific point.

You’ve already been through one compaction (the long summary at the start of this context), and we’re creeping back toward 50% context again. Strategy only — **no code**. Grounded in the current architecture, the monetization/PMF docs, and a 41-source external research pass.\n\n## Recommended architecture (headlines)\n\n- **Layered control-plane / data-plane split** — identity, ingestion, storage, orchestration, execution/compute, the (existing) pipeline, and per-user data-serving, each behind a small interface.\n- **Two new pluggable seams** mirroring the existing registry pattern:\n  - **`StorageBackend`** — build over **`fsspec`** (+ `smart_open` for large-MP4 streaming) for local/Drive/S3/GCS/Azure; **BYO-storage** via **STS `AssumeRole` + presigned URLs** so raw video moves browser↔the user\u0026#39;s bucket directly and we never store their long-lived keys.\n  - **`ComputeBackend`** — local / hosted GPU / **BYO-VM via an outbound pull-based worker** (GitHub self-hosted-runner pattern) so the orchestrator never holds the user\u0026#39;s cloud creds; **SkyPilot** for on-demand GPU in the user\u0026#39;s own cloud.\n- **Coach-first multi-tenant data model** (org/academy → coach → athlete → user); **highlights become a first-class persisted entity** + per-user history. Recommend **Postgres + `tenant_id` + Row-Level Security** (hosted); SQLite-per-tenant for self-hosted single-academy.\n- **Orchestration: Hatchet** (Postgres-backed durable jobs, resumable long GPU jobs) — the multi-user superset of `DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md` #16.\n- **Privacy:** the load-bearing nuance — **shot/highlight detection = general analytics (not Art. 9); gait that builds a per-athlete identifying template = Art. 9 biometric** needing explicit consent; minors/parental consent for academies; **BYO-storage as the liability lever**; **Gemini via Vertex AI** (no-train), not the consumer tier.\n- **Differentiator (validated):** none of Veo/Hudl/SwingVision/Spiideo offer true BYO-storage/compute (SwingVision does *edge* phone compute) — **\u0026#34;your video, your storage, your compute\u0026#34;** is genuinely novel for privacy-sensitive academies.\n\n## Phased roadmap\nP0 async + Postgres/RLS tenancy → P1 multi-user + upload + highlight persistence + history → P2 `StorageBackend` + BYO-storage → P3 `ComputeBackend` + BYO-compute → P4 assistant-coach (shot detection, then gait behind consent). The doc ends with the **key decisions left to you** and risks.\n\n## Notes\n- Docs-only; wired into `docs/README.md` and `COMMERCIALIZATION.md` C12. 41 cited sources (fact-vs-synthesis tagged; validate before budget).\n- Branched off `master`, **no stacking** (one PR, one work item).\n- Does not start implementation; the first infra slice (async, #16) is still on hold pending your review of #53.\n\nhttps://claude.ai/code/session_01DKXGwTeJi6BDdvZgEV5JSA\n\n---\n_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code/session_01DKXGwTeJi6BDdvZgEV5JSA)_","state":"open","draft":false,"merged":false,"mergeable_state":"clean","html_url":"https://github.com/avidullu/badminton-highlight-indexer/pull/58","user":{"login":"avidullu","id":1674877,"profile_url":"https://github.com/avidullu","avatar_url":"https://avatars.githubusercontent.com/u/1674877?v=4"},"head":{"ref":"claude/platform-architecture-research","sha":"bc7b99ecbdc9fc0f216cbb5162df3ad523b3b8fe","repo":{"full_name":"avidullu/badminton-highlight-indexer"}},"base":{"ref":"master","sha":"5f7e97a7d0f92c26b551c985f5639410f1f5199c","repo":{"full_name":"avidullu/badminton-highlight-indexer"}},"additions":559,"deletions":16,"changed_files":3,"commits":8,"created_at":"2026-06-07T19:35:01Z","updated_at":"2026-06-08T05:24:14Z"}

Keep new deps permissive. |\n \n ---\n "},{"filename":"docs/PLATFORM_ARCHITECTURE.md","status":"added","additions":537,"changes":537,"patch":"@@ -0,0 +1,537 @@\n+# Platform Architecture — Multi-User \"Assistant Coach\"\n+\n+**Status:** 📐 Strategy / research — for review. No code yet.\n+**Date:** 2026-06-07 · **Owner:** avidullu\n+\n+**Purpose.** Map the path from today's **single-user, local** tool to a **multi-tenant\n+\"assistant coach\" platform**: users upload videos, get highlights **stored and\n+browsable by history**, and choose **where their data lives** (Google Drive / cloud\n+bucket / local) and **where the compute runs** (our GPU / their VM). The organizing\n+principle is to **decouple the layers** — identity, ingestion, storage, orchestration,\n+execution/compute, the detection+highlight pipeline, and data-serving — behind small\n+**pluggable interfaces**, reusing the registry pattern the codebase already uses for\n+segmenters/providers/validators/sports/annotations.\n+\n+**Product framing (the north star).** This is an **assistant coach**, not just a\n+clipper. Highlights are the *entry point*; the *target* is **shot detection + gait /\n+biomechanics feedback under a supervising coach**. The buyer is the **academy/coach**\n+(see the beachhead in [`PMF_MARKET_RESEARCH.md`](PMF_MARKET_RESEARCH.md)); **power users\n+self-serve**. Two consequences ripple through this whole design: (1) the data model is\n+**coach → athlete**, not just \"a user with videos\"; (2) gait/biomechanics is **biometric\n+data**, which raises the privacy bar (see §7).\n+\n+\u003e **Competitive note (validated by research):** none of Veo / Hudl / SwingVision /\n+\u003e Spiideo offer true BYO-storage or BYO-compute — they're vendor-cloud SaaS\n+\u003e (SwingVision uniquely runs *edge* compute on the user's iPhone). So **\"your video,\n+\u003e your storage, your compute\"** is a genuine differentiator for privacy-sensitive\n+\u003e academies — and SwingVision proves user-side compute is commercially viable. All four\n+\u003e share the same **teams → athletes → clips** data model, which we adopt (§4).\n+\n+\u003e Scope: this is a **direction-setting doc**. It does not start implementation; the\n+\u003e first infra slice (an async job model) is the on-hold #16 in\n+\u003e [`DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md`](DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md). Commercial constraints come\n+\u003e from [`COMMERCIALIZATION.md`](COMMERCIALIZATION.md) (C4 paid-LLM no-train; C12 SaaS\n+\u003e foundations) and `archives/MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md`.\n+\n+## 1. Current state \u0026 gaps (what we're evolving from)\n+\n+| Concern | Today | Gap for multi-user |\n+|---|---|---|\n+| Identity / tenancy | none | no users, orgs, auth, or isolation |\n+| Storage | local `filepath` string; output to `output_dir` | no abstraction; can't BYO Drive/bucket; raw video must be on the box |\n+| Data model | `videos` keyed by file **MD5**, `play_segments`, `events`, `candidate_segments` (SQLite) | no owner/athlete link; **highlights aren't a first-class persisted entity** (compile writes a file and returns a path); no per-user history |\n+| Execution | pipeline runs **in-process, synchronously** in `/api/process` | blocks; can't dispatch to a user's VM/GPU; no job status |\n+| Pipeline | **already pluggable** (segmenter/provider/validator/sport/annotation registries) | reuse as-is — this layer is the model for the rest |\n+| API | `/api/process`, `/api/query`, `/api/compile`, `/api/config`; no auth | needs auth, per-tenant scoping, async job + history endpoints |\n+\n+Grounding: `backend/infrastructure/database.py` (schema + `PRAGMA user_version`\n+migration ladder, v1), `backend/main.py` (endpoints, sync flow), the registry pattern\n+in `backend/pipeline/segmenters/base.py::SegmenterRegistry` and\n+`backend/providers/base.py`.\n+\n+## 2. Target layered architecture\n+\n+Decouple into layers with a clean **control-plane vs data-plane** split — the\n+orchestrator coordinates *metadata and jobs*; the heavy *bytes and GPU work* can live\n+elsewhere (even in the user's own cloud).\n+\n+```\n+                ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n+   CONTROL      │  Presentation / API  (auth, per-tenant)        │\n+   PLANE        │  Identity \u0026 Tenancy  (org→coach→athlete→user)  │\n+                │  Orchestration / Jobs (submit→status→result)   │\n+                └───────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────┘\n+                                │ dispatch       │ metadata\n+                ┌───────────────▼───────┐  ┌─────▼───────────────┐\n+   DATA         │  Execution / Compute  │  │  Data / Serving     │\n+   PLANE        │  (local | BYO-VM |    │  │  (per-tenant DB:    │\n+                │   hosted GPU)         │  │   videos, segments, │\n+                │   runs the Pipeline:  │  │   highlights, jobs, │\n+                │   detect → highlight  │  │   history)          │\n+                └───────────┬───────────┘  └─────────────────────┘\n+                            │ read input / write artifacts\n+                ┌───────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐\n+                │  Storage  (local upload | Google Drive | S3 |  │\n+                │  GCS | Azure)  — raw video + highlight outputs │\n+                └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n+```\n+\n+The two **new pluggable seams** are **Storage** and **Compute/Execution**; everything\n+else either exists (pipeline) or is net-new infra (identity, orchestration, serving). A\n+**model/training layer** — the owned generative video-QA model (§5A) — sits atop these,\n+fed by Storage (training corpus), Data (labels), and Compute (training + inference), and\n+is delivered through the existing **AI-provider registry with a paid fallback**.\n+\n+## 3. Pluggable abstractions (extend the existing registry pattern)\n+\n+The codebase already proves the model: an `ABC` + a `Registry` singleton with\n+`register/get/list_available` (e.g. `SegmenterRegistry`). Add two more registries with\n+the same shape so they're plug-and-play and testable in isolation.\n+\n+### 3.1 `StorageBackend` registry\n+Replace the bare `video_path: str` that flows through the pipeline with a\n+**`StorageRef`** (a backend id + a key/URI), resolved by a backend.\n+\n+```python\n+class StorageBackend(ABC):\n+    name: str\n+    def open_read(self, ref: StorageRef) -\u003e BinaryIO: ...      # stream large video\n+    def download_to_local(self, ref: StorageRef, dst: str): ...  # for ffmpeg/cv2/WSL\n+    def upload(self, local_path: str, ref: StorageRef): ...     # persist highlight/artifacts\n+    def signed_url(self, ref: StorageRef, ttl: int) -\u003e str: ... # hand to LLM / browser\n+    def stat(self, ref: StorageRef) -\u003e StorageStat: ...\n+    def list(self, prefix: StorageRef) -\u003e list[StorageRef]: ...\n+```\n+\n+- **Recommended implementation:** build the backend over **`fsspec`** (one matrix —\n+  local / S3 (`s3fs`) / GCS (`gcsfs`) / Azure (`adlfs`) + Google Drive via `gdrivefs`,\n+  Dropbox via `dropboxdrivefs`), reaching for **`smart_open`** as the robust large-MP4\n+  byte-streaming primitive. Storage becomes one more registry. (Treat Drive/Dropbox as\n+  add-ons — less battle-tested than `s3fs`/`gcsfs`; cover with integration tests.)\n+- **Backends:** `local` (upload dir), `gdrive`, `s3`, `gcs`, `azure`. **BYO-storage**:\n+  the tenant registers their own bucket/Drive; raw video **never leaves their account**.\n+- **Auth — never persist users' long-lived keys.** For buckets, have the user create an\n+  IAM role you **`AssumeRole`** into, scoped to one bucket/prefix, and mint **presigned\n+  URLs** so raw video moves **browser ↔ the user's bucket directly, never through our\n+  servers**. For Drive, OAuth with the narrow **`drive.file`** scope; for academies, a\n+  **service account with domain-wide delegation**. Store any per-tenant creds encrypted.\n+- **Why it matters for the pipeline:** ffmpeg/OpenCV/WSL need a **local file**, while the\n+  LLM provider can take a **signed URL**. The backend exposes both `download_to_local`\n+  (for compute) and `signed_url` (for the provider/browser), so the pipeline stops caring\n+  *where* the bytes live.\n+- **Migration path:** introduce `StorageRef` alongside the current local path; a `local`\n+  backend wraps today's behavior so nothing breaks; adapters land incrementally.\n+\n+### 3.2 `ComputeBackend` / `ExecutionBackend` registry\n+Where the pipeline actually runs for a given job.\n+\n+- **`local`** — in-process / local subprocess (today's behavior; dev + self-host).\n+- **`hosted`** — our GPU VM pool (the SaaS default).\n+- **`byo_worker`** — the tenant runs a **pull-based worker** (a small agent) on their own\n+  VM/GPU. **Critical security property:** the worker *pulls* jobs scoped to its tenant\n+  from the control plane, pulls input from the tenant's **own** storage, runs the\n+  pipeline, and writes artifacts + results back — so **the orchestrator never holds the\n+  user's cloud credentials** and needs **no inbound access** to their network. This\n+  mirrors the **self-hosted CI runner** pattern (GitHub Actions runners use a 50s\n+  outbound-only HTTPS long-poll). This pull-based runner is the **cheapest, most\n+  credential-safe v1** for BYO-compute.\n+- For users who want the platform to **provision GPUs in their *own* cloud account on\n+  demand** (an academy with an AWS account but no standing GPU box), add **SkyPilot**\n+  (BYOC: everything launches in the user's accounts/VPCs; creds stay with them).\n+  **Modal** is the managed fallback for users wanting zero infra (accepting that raw\n+  video then flows through Modal).\n+\n+The pipeline code is unchanged; only *where it's invoked* and *how it gets bytes* vary.\n+\n+### 3.3 Reuse, don't reinvent\n+The existing `segmenter` / `provider` / `validator` / `sport` / `annotation` registries\n+are the proven precedent and stay as the **pipeline** layer. The `annotation` registry +\n+`candidate_segments` table are also the natural seam for the **coach-correction / golden\n+-set** loop in the assistant-coach phase (§6, P4).\n+\n+## 4. Multi-tenant data model \u0026 serving layer\n+\n+### 4.1 Entities — generic core, with the coach model as an optional overlay\n+The foundational schema is deliberately **domain-neutral** so we don't overfit to the\n+coach mindset — the coach/athlete framing is a **market-fit hypothesis t

@@ -0,0 +1,537 @@
+# Platform Architecture — Multi-User "Assistant Coach"
+
+**Status:** 📐 Strategy / research — for review. No code yet.
+**Date:** 2026-06-07 · **Owner:** avidullu
+
+**Purpose.** Map the path from today's **single-user, local** tool to a **multi-tenant
+"assistant coach" platform**: users upload videos, get highlights **stored and
+browsable by history**, and choose **where their data lives** (Google Drive / cloud
+bucket / local) and **where the compute runs** (our GPU / their VM). The organizing
+principle is to **decouple the layers** — identity, ingestion, storage, orchestration,
+execution/compute, the detection+highlight pipeline, and data-serving — behind small
+**pluggable interfaces**, reusing the registry pattern the codebase already uses for
+segmenters/providers/validators/sports/annotations.
+
+**Product framing (the north star).** This is an **assistant coach**, not just a
+clipper. Highlights are the *entry point*; the *target* is **shot detection + gait /
+biomechanics feedback under a supervising coach**. The buyer is the **academy/coach**
+(see the beachhead in [`PMF_MARKET_RESEARCH.md`](PMF_MARKET_RESEARCH.md)); **power users
+self-serve**. Two consequences ripple through this whole design: (1) the data model is
+**coach → athlete**, not just "a user with videos"; (2) gait/biomechanics is **biometric
+data**, which raises the privacy bar (see §7).
+
+> **Competitive note (validated by research):** none of Veo / Hudl / SwingVision /
+> Spiideo offer true BYO-storage or BYO-compute — they're vendor-cloud SaaS
+> (SwingVision uniquely runs *edge* compute on the user's iPhone). So **"your video,
+> your storage, your compute"** is a genuine differentiator for privacy-sensitive
+> academies — and SwingVision proves user-side compute is commercially viable. All four
+> share the same **teams → athletes → clips** data model, which we adopt (§4).
+
+> Scope: this is a **direction-setting doc**. It does not start implementation; the
+> first infra slice (an async job model) is the on-hold #16 in
+> [`DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md`](DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md). Commercial constraints come
+> from [`COMMERCIALIZATION.md`](COMMERCIALIZATION.md) (C4 paid-LLM no-train; C12 SaaS
+> foundations) and `archives/MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md`.
+
+## 1. Current state & gaps (what we're evolving from)
+
+| Concern | Today | Gap for multi-user |
+|---|---|---|
+| Identity / tenancy | none | no users, orgs, auth, or isolation |
+| Storage | local `filepath` string; output to `output_dir` | no abstraction; can't BYO Drive/bucket; raw video must be on the box |
+| Data model | `videos` keyed by file **MD5**, `play_segments`, `events`, `candidate_segments` (SQLite) | no owner/athlete link; **highlights aren't a first-class persisted entity** (compile writes a file and returns a path); no per-user history |
+| Execution | pipeline runs **in-process, synchronously** in `/api/process` | blocks; can't dispatch to a user's VM/GPU; no job status |
+| Pipeline | **already pluggable** (segmenter/provider/validator/sport/annotation registries) | reuse as-is — this layer is the model for the rest |
+| API | `/api/process`, `/api/query`, `/api/compile`, `/api/config`; no auth | needs auth, per-tenant scoping, async job + history endpoints |
+
+Grounding: `backend/infrastructure/database.py` (schema + `PRAGMA user_version`
+migration ladder, v1), `backend/main.py` (endpoints, sync flow), the registry pattern
+in `backend/pipeline/segmenters/base.py::SegmenterRegistry` and
+`backend/providers/base.py`.
+
+## 2. Target layered architecture
+
+Decouple into layers with a clean **control-plane vs data-plane** split — the
+orchestrator coordinates *metadata and jobs*; the heavy *bytes and GPU work* can live
+elsewhere (even in the user's own cloud).
+
+```
+                ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
+   CONTROL      │  Presentation / API  (auth, per-tenant)        │
+   PLANE        │  Identity & Tenancy  (org→coach→athlete→user)  │
+                │  Orchestration / Jobs (submit→status→result)   │
+                └───────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────┘
+                                │ dispatch       │ metadata
+                ┌───────────────▼───────┐  ┌─────▼───────────────┐
+   DATA         │  Execution / Compute  │  │  Data / Serving     │
+   PLANE        │  (local | BYO-VM |    │  │  (per-tenant DB:    │
+                │   hosted GPU)         │  │   videos, segments, │
+                │   runs the Pipeline:  │  │   highlights, jobs, │
+                │   detect → highlight  │  │   history)          │
+                └───────────┬───────────┘  └─────────────────────┘
+                            │ read input / write artifacts
+                ┌───────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐
+                │  Storage  (local upload | Google Drive | S3 |  │
+                │  GCS | Azure)  — raw video + highlight outputs │
+                └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
+```
+
+The two **new pluggable seams** are **Storage** and **Compute/Execution**; everything
+else either exists (pipeline) or is net-new infra (identity, orchestration, serving). A
+**model/training layer** — the owned generative video-QA model (§5A) — sits atop these,
+fed by Storage (training corpus), Data (labels), and Compute (training + inference), and
+is delivered through the existing **AI-provider registry with a paid fallback**.
+
+## 3. Pluggable abstractions (extend the existing registry pattern)
+
+The codebase already proves the model: an `ABC` + a `Registry` singleton with
+`register/get/list_available` (e.g. `SegmenterRegistry`). Add two more registries with
+the same shape so they're plug-and-play and testable in isolation.
+
+### 3.1 `StorageBackend` registry
+Replace the bare `video_path: str` that flows through the pipeline with a
+**`StorageRef`** (a backend id + a key/URI), resolved by a backend.
+
+```python
+class StorageBackend(ABC):
+    name: str
+    def open_read(self, ref: StorageRef) -> BinaryIO: ...      # stream large video
+    def download_to_local(self, ref: StorageRef, dst: str): ...  # for ffmpeg/cv2/WSL
+    def upload(self, local_path: str, ref: StorageRef): ...     # persist highlight/artifacts
+    def signed_url(self, ref: StorageRef, ttl: int) -> str: ... # hand to LLM / browser
+    def stat(self, ref: StorageRef) -> StorageStat: ...
+    def list(self, prefix: StorageRef) -> list[StorageRef]: ...
+```
+
+- **Recommended implementation:** build the backend over **`fsspec`** (one matrix —
+  local / S3 (`s3fs`) / GCS (`gcsfs`) / Azure (`adlfs`) + Google Drive via `gdrivefs`,
+  Dropbox via `dropboxdrivefs`), reaching for **`smart_open`** as the robust large-MP4
+  byte-streaming primitive. Storage becomes one more registry. (Treat Drive/Dropbox as
+  add-ons — less battle-tested than `s3fs`/`gcsfs`; cover with integration tests.)
+- **Backends:** `local` (upload dir), `gdrive`, `s3`, `gcs`, `azure`. **BYO-storage**:
+  the tenant registers their own bucket/Drive; raw video **never leaves their account**.
+- **Auth — never persist users' long-lived keys.** For buckets, have the user create an
+  IAM role you **`AssumeRole`** into, scoped to one bucket/prefix, and mint **presigned
+  URLs** so raw video moves **browser ↔ the user's bucket directly, never through our
+  servers**. For Drive, OAuth with the narrow **`drive.file`** scope; for academies, a
+  **service account with domain-wide delegation**. Store any per-tenant creds encrypted.
+- **Why it matters for the pipeline:** ffmpeg/OpenCV/WSL need a **local file**, while the
+  LLM provider can take a **signed URL**. The backend exposes both `download_to_local`
+  (for compute) and `signed_url` (for the provider/browser), so the pipeline stops caring
+  *where* the bytes live.
+- **Migration path:** introduce `StorageRef` alongside the current local path; a `local`
+  backend wraps today's behavior so nothing breaks; adapters land incrementally.
+
+### 3.2 `ComputeBackend` / `ExecutionBackend` registry
+Where the pipeline actually runs for a given job.
+
+- **`local`** — in-process / local subprocess (today's behavior; dev + self-host).
+- **`hosted`** — our GPU VM pool (the SaaS default).
+- **`byo_worker`** — the tenant runs a **pull-based worker** (a small agent) on their own
+  VM/GPU. **Critical security property:** the worker *pulls* jobs scoped to its tenant
+  from the control plane, pulls input from the tenant's **own** storage, runs the
+  pipeline, and writes artifacts + results back — so **the orchestrator never holds the
+  user's cloud credentials** and needs **no inbound access** to their network. This
+  mirrors the **self-hosted CI runner** pattern (GitHub Actions runners use a 50s
+  outbound-only HTTPS long-poll). This pull-based runner is the **cheapest, most
+  credential-safe v1** for BYO-compute.
+- For users who want the platform to **provision GPUs in their *own* cloud account on
+  demand** (an academy with an AWS account but no standing GPU box), add **SkyPilot**
+  (BYOC: everything launches in the user's accounts/VPCs; creds stay with them).
+  **Modal** is the managed fallback for users wanting zero infra (accepting that raw
+  video then flows through Modal).
+
+The pipeline code is unchanged; only *where it's invoked* and *how it gets bytes* vary.
+
+### 3.3 Reuse, don't reinvent
+The existing `segmenter` / `provider` / `validator` / `sport` / `annotation` registries
+are the proven precedent and stay as the **pipeline** layer. The `annotation` registry +
+`candidate_segments` table are also the natural seam for the **coach-correction / golden
+-set** loop in the assistant-coach phase (§6, P4).
+
+## 4. Multi-tenant data model & serving layer
+
+### 4.1 Entities — generic core, with the coach model as an optional overlay
+The foundational schema is deliberately **domain-neutral** so we don't overfit to the
+coach mindset — the coach/athlete framing is a **market-fit hypothesis that will iterate**
+(see C13 / PMF), not a load-bearing assumption. **Generic core:**
+```
+User ──< VideoCollection ──< Video ──< Highlight ──< HighlightMetadata
+

# Platform Architecture — Multi-User "Assistant Coach"

**Status:** 📐 Strategy / research — for review. No code yet.
**Date:** 2026-06-07 · **Owner:** avidullu

**Purpose.** Map the path from today's **single-user, local** tool to a **multi-tenant
"assistant coach" platform**: users upload videos, get highlights **stored and
browsable by history**, and choose **where their data lives** (Google Drive / cloud
bucket / local) and **where the compute runs** (our GPU / their VM). The organizing
principle is to **decouple the layers** — identity, ingestion, storage, orchestration,
execution/compute, the detection+highlight pipeline, and data-serving — behind small
**pluggable interfaces**, reusing the registry pattern the codebase already uses for
segmenters/providers/validators/sports/annotations.

**Product framing (the north star).** This is an **assistant coach**, not just a
clipper. Highlights are the *entry point*; the *target* is **shot detection + gait /
biomechanics feedback under a supervising coach**. The buyer is the **academy/coach**
(see the beachhead in [`PMF_MARKET_RESEARCH.md`](PMF_MARKET_RESEARCH.md)); **power users
self-serve**. Two consequences ripple through this whole design: (1) the data model is
**coach → athlete**, not just "a user with videos"; (2) gait/biomechanics is **biometric
data**, which raises the privacy bar (see §7).

> **Competitive note (validated by research):** none of Veo / Hudl / SwingVision /
> Spiideo offer true BYO-storage or BYO-compute — they're vendor-cloud SaaS
> (SwingVision uniquely runs *edge* compute on the user's iPhone). So **"your video,
> your storage, your compute"** is a genuine differentiator for privacy-sensitive
> academies — and SwingVision proves user-side compute is commercially viable. All four
> share the same **teams → athletes → clips** data model, which we adopt (§4).

> Scope: this is a **direction-setting doc**. It does not start implementation; the
> first infra slice (an async job model) is the on-hold #16 in
> [`DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md`](DEFERRED_HARDENING_PLAN.md). Commercial constraints come
> from [`COMMERCIALIZATION.md`](COMMERCIALIZATION.md) (C4 paid-LLM no-train; C12 SaaS
> foundations) and `archives/MONETIZATION_AUDIT.md`.

## 1. Current state & gaps (what we're evolving from)

| Concern | Today | Gap for multi-user |
|---|---|---|
| Identity / tenancy | none | no users, orgs, auth, or isolation |
| Storage | local `filepath` string; output to `output_dir` | no abstraction; can't BYO Drive/bucket; raw video must be on the box |
| Data model | `videos` keyed by file **MD5**, `play_segments`, `events`, `candidate_segments` (SQLite) | no owner/athlete link; **highlights aren't a first-class persisted entity** (compile writes a file and returns a path); no per-user history |
| Execution | pipeline runs **in-process, synchronously** in `/api/process` | blocks; can't dispatch to a user's VM/GPU; no job status |
| Pipeline | **already pluggable** (segmenter/provider/validator/sport/annotation registries) | reuse as-is — this layer is the model for the rest |
| API | `/api/process`, `/api/query`, `/api/compile`, `/api/config`; no auth | needs auth, per-tenant scoping, async job + history endpoints |

Grounding: `backend/infrastructure/database.py` (schema + `PRAGMA user_version`
migration ladder, v1), `backend/main.py` (endpoints, sync flow), the registry pattern
in `backend/pipeline/segmenters/base.py::SegmenterRegistry` and
`backend/providers/base.py`.

## 2. Target layered architecture

Decouple into layers with a clean **control-plane vs data-plane** split — the
orchestrator coordinates *metadata and jobs*; the heavy *bytes and GPU work* can live
elsewhere (even in the user's own cloud).

```
                ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   CONTROL      │  Presentation / API  (auth, per-tenant)        │
   PLANE        │  Identity & Tenancy  (org→coach→athlete→user)  │
                │  Orchestration / Jobs (submit→status→result)   │
                └───────────────┬───────────────┬────────────────┘
                                │ dispatch       │ metadata
                ┌───────────────▼───────┐  ┌─────▼───────────────┐
   DATA         │  Execution / Compute  │  │  Data / Serving     │
   PLANE        │  (local | BYO-VM |    │  │  (per-tenant DB:    │
                │   hosted GPU)         │  │   videos, segments, │
                │   runs the Pipeline:  │  │   highlights, jobs, │
                │   detect → highlight  │  │   history)          │
                └───────────┬───────────┘  └─────────────────────┘
                            │ read input / write artifacts
                ┌───────────▼───────────────────────────────────┐
                │  Storage  (local upload | Google Drive | S3 |  │
                │  GCS | Azure)  — raw video + highlight outputs │
                └────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The two **new pluggable seams** are **Storage** and **Compute/Execution**; everything
else either exists (pipeline) or is net-new infra (identity, orchestration, serving). A
**model/training layer** — the owned generative video-QA model (§5A) — sits atop these,
fed by Storage (training corpus), Data (labels), and Compute (training + inference), and
is delivered through the existing **AI-provider registry with a paid fallback**.

## 3. Pluggable abstractions (extend the existing registry pattern)

The codebase already proves the model: an `ABC` + a `Registry` singleton with
`register/get/list_available` (e.g. `SegmenterRegistry`). Add two more registries with
the same shape so they're plug-and-play and testable in isolation.

### 3.1 `StorageBackend` registry
Replace the bare `video_path: str` that flows through the pipeline with a
**`StorageRef`** (a backend id + a key/URI), resolved by a backend.

```python
class StorageBackend(ABC):
    name: str
    def open_read(self, ref: StorageRef) -> BinaryIO: ...      # stream large video
    def download_to_local(self, ref: StorageRef, dst: str): ...  # for ffmpeg/cv2/WSL
    def upload(self, local_path: str, ref: StorageRef): ...     # persist highlight/artifacts

### 259. assistant

**Deep Review of PR #58: "docs: multi-user 'assistant coach' platform architecture strategy"**

**TL;DR**  
This is a high-quality, well-researched **direction-setting strategy document**. It is the natural next layer after the scaffolding work we've been shipping (#49–#51 + the recent docs declutter). The core thesis — **decouple control plane from data plane, make storage and compute pluggable/BYO, treat "assistant coach" (not just clipper) as the product north star** — is coherent with the existing registry-heavy architecture and the commercialization constraints.

It is **not** over-optimistic vaporware; it explicitly scopes itself as non-implementation and correctly defers the first real slice (async + #16 in the hardening plan) until you approve direction.

**I recommend merging** after addressing a few clarifications and tightening. The commercialization dashboard update is a reasonable consequence of this thinking (it surfaces the right new P0/P1 items).

### Strengths

1. **Excellent reuse of existing patterns**  
   The proposal to add `StorageBackend` and `ComputeBackend` registries that mirror `SegmenterRegistry` / `ProviderRegistry` is the single best architectural decision in the doc. It keeps the "pipeline" layer (the thing that actually does detection) unchanged and isolated. This is disciplined.

2. **Privacy & liability thinking is unusually strong**  
   Calling out that **gait/biomechanics = biometric (Art. 9)** while shot/highlight detection is "just analytics" is exactly the kind of nuance that kills or saves a sports-tech company. The "BYO-storage as the liability lever" + "never store long-lived keys" + presigned URLs + pull-based workers is the correct threat model. The SwingVision edge-compute reference as existence proof is smart.

3. **Competitive differentiation is grounded**  
   The repeated emphasis that Veo/Hudl/SwingVision/Spiideo do *not* offer true BYO-storage/compute (SwingVision's phone edge case is correctly caveated) is the real wedge. This is not generic "we'll do multi-tenant."

4. **Phasing and scoping discipline**  
   The P0→P4 roadmap is realistic. Starting with async + basic tenancy before touching the sexy pluggable backends is correct. The explicit call-out that this does not start code (and that #16 is still blocked pending your review of #53) is responsible.

5. **Data model evolution is thoughtful**  
   The "generic core + coach overlay" + making `Highlight` a first-class persisted entity (instead of "we just wrote a file") is the right modeling move. The MD5-keyed `videos` table was always going to have to evolve for multi-user.

### Concerns & Open Questions

**1. Operational complexity of the BYO-worker model (the biggest practical risk)**  
The pull-based worker pattern is elegant on paper (no inbound, no creds in orchestrator). But for an Indian academy coach who is not a DevOps person, "run this small agent on your VM/GPU" can become a support nightmare. 

Have you considered a "zero-infra BYO" tier that still goes through a narrow, audited proxy (with strict egress controls and per-tenant isolation), or is the stance "if they want BYO they must be willing to run the worker"?

**2. Migration path for existing single-user data is underspecified**  
Current users have SQLite DBs with MD5-keyed videos. The doc talks about the target model but not the transition:
- How do we attach ownership/athlete identity to existing videos?
- Do we do a one-time "import" that treats the current local user as the first coach?
- What happens to already-compiled highlight files that live only in `output/`?

This is solvable, but the doc should have a short "Migration & coexistence" subsection.

**3. Orchestration choice (Hatchet) feels early**  
Hatchet is interesting (Postgres + durable execution), but it is still relatively young. The doc positions it as "the multi-user superset of the deferred hardening plan." Before we commit, we should at least see a short comparison against:
- Temporal (more mature, but heavier)
- A simple Postgres-backed job queue + worker (lighter, what most people actually ship first)
- The existing deferred plan's async design (#16)

Even if Hatchet wins, the choice should be justified.

**4. The "owned model" thread (C4 + C14 + §5A) is getting complicated**  
The platform doc + the updated commercialization table are now tightly coupled on the idea of a fine-tuned open VLM (Qwen2.5-VL etc.) as primary, with paid Gemini as fallback. 

The new C14 constraints (Apache-2.0 bases only, never train on paid-LLM outputs, explicit consent ledger, exclude minors) are correct but extremely high-bar. The doc should be explicit about the **minimum viable first owned model** (e.g. "shot classification only, not full generative QA") so we don't accidentally commit to training a full video-QA model before the flywheel has real consented data.

**5. Scope creep risk on the "assistant coach" vision**  
The north star (shot detection → gait/biomechanics under coach supervision) is exciting and correct for the target buyer. However, the doc moves from "highlights are the entry point" to full biometric feedback very quickly. 

I would like a clearer "Phase 4 is deliberately narrow at first" statement (e.g. "first version of assistant coach is shot-count + basic classification feedback only; full gait template is Phase 4b and requires separate consent").

### Specific Suggestions for the Document

- Add a short **"Threat model & failure modes"** subsection under the BYO-worker and StorageBackend sections.
- Add a **"Migration & coexistence"** paragraph (even 4–5 bullets).
- Make the Hatchet choice more explicit (or turn it into a decision to be validated in the first async slice).
- In the commercialization dashboard update, the new grouping (P0/P1/P2 + Pending/In-flight/Completed) is clearer than the old one. Good change, but consider keeping the old C# numbers stable as you did.
- The 41-source research is impressive. A one-sentence note on methodology ("41 sources, fact vs. synthesis tagged") is good; consider adding a short "Key sources we are *not* relying on and why" if there were strong competing architectures.

### Bottom Line / Recommendation

This is one of the better strategy docs I've seen in this repo. It correctly identifies that the hard part of going multi-user here is **not** the detection pipeline (we already made that pluggable) but the **control-plane / data-plane decoupling + credential isolation + privacy model**.

My vote is **approve and merge**, with the request that the author add the migration note and a short "why Hatchet (or equivalent durable execution) vs. lighter alternatives" paragraph before we treat the async slice (#16) as unblocked.

Would you like me to draft specific review comments you could post on the PR (phrased neutrally and actionably), or would you prefer I expand on any of the points above (especially the operational complexity of BYO or the owned-model constraints)? 

I'm also happy to cross-check any specific section against the current codebase (e.g. how hard it would actually be to introduce `StorageRef` alongside the existing `video_path` string).
